Multiparty Session Programming with Global Protocol Combinators
Keigo Imai, Rumyana Neykova, Nobuko Yoshida, Shoji Yuen

Abstract
Multiparty Session Types (MPST) is a typing discipline for communication protocols. It ensures the absence of communication errors and deadlocks for well-typed communicating processes. The state-of-the-art implementations of the MPST theory rely on (1) runtime linearity checks to ensure correct usage of communication channels and (2) external domain-specific languages for specifying and verifying multiparty protocols. To overcome these limitations, we propose a library for programming with global combinators – a set of functions for writing and verifying multiparty protocols in OCaml. Local behaviours for all processes in a protocol are inferred at once from a global combinator. We formalise global combinators and prove a sound realisability of global combinators – a well-typed global combinator derives a set of local types, by which typed endpoint programs can ensure type and communication safety. Our approach enables fully-static verification and implementation of the whole protocol, from the protocol specification to the process implementations, to happen in the same language. We compare our implementation to untyped and continuation-passing style implementations, and demonstrate its expressiveness by implementing a plethora of protocols. We show our library can interoperate with existing libraries and services, implementing DNS (Domain Name Service) protocol and the OAuth (Open Authentication) protocol.

1
Introduction

Multiparty Session Types.
Multiparty Session Types (MPST)
[
27
,
12
,
28
]
is a theoretical framework that stipulates how to write, verify and ensure correct implementations of communication protocols.
The methodology of programming with MPST (depicted in Fig.
1
(a)) starts from a communication protocol (a
global type
) which specifies the behaviour of a system of interacting processes. The local behaviour (a
local type
)
for each endpoint process is then algorithmically
projected
from the
protocol. Finally, each endpoint process is implemented in an endpoint host language and type-checked against its respective local type by a session
typing system. The guarantee of session types is that a system of well-typed endpoint
processes
does not go wrong
, i.e it does not exhibit
communication errors such as reception errors, orphan messages or deadlocks, and satisfies
session fidelity, i.e. the local behaviour of each process follows the global specification.

The theoretical MPST framework ensures desirable safety properties. In practice,
session types implementations that enforce these properties
statically
, i.e at compile-time, are limited to binary (two party protocols)

[
50
,
45
,
37
,
47
]
.
Extending binary session types
implementations
to multiparty interactions, which support static linearity checks
(i.e., linear usage of channels),
is non-trivial, and poses two implementation challenges.

(C1) How global types can be specified and verified in a general-purpose programming language?
Checking compatibility of two communicating processes relies on
duality
, i.e., when one process performs an action, the other performs a complementary (dual) action. Checking the compatibility of multiple processes is more complicated, and
relies on the existence of a
well-formed
global protocol and the syntax-directed procedure of
projection
,
which derives local types from a global specification. A global protocol is considered
well-formed
,
if local types can be derived via projection.
Since global types are far from the types of a “mainstream” programming language,
state-of-the-art MPST implementations

[
29
,
42
,
54
,
10
]
use
external domain-specific protocol description languages and tools
(e.g. the Scribble toolchain
[
57
]
)
to specify global types and to implement the verification procedure of projection.
The usage of external tools for protocol description and verification
widens the gap between the specification and
its implementations and makes it more difficult to locate protocol violations
in the program, i.e. the correspondence between an error in the program and
the protocol is less apparent.

(C2) How to implement safe multiparty communication over binary channels?

The theory of MPST requires processes to communicate over multiparty channels – channels that carry messages between two or more parties; their types stipulate the
precise sequencing of the communication between multiple processes. Additionally, multiparty channels has to be used linearly, i.e exactly once.
In practice, however, (1) communication channels are binary, i.e a TCP socket for example connects only two parties, and hence its type can describe interactions between two entities only; (2) most languages do not support typing of linear resources. Existing MPST implementations

[
29
,
42
,
54
,
10
]
apply two workarounds. To preserve the order of interactions when implementing a multiparty protocol over binary channels, existing works use code generation (e.g.
[
57
]
) and generate local types (APIs) for several (nominal) programming languages. Note that although the interactions order is preserved, most of these implementations
[
29
,
42
,
10
]
still require type-casts on the underlying channels, compromising type safety of the host type system.
To ensure linear usage of multiparty channels,
runtime checks
are inserted to detect if a channel has been used more than once.
This is because the type systems of their respective host languages do not provide static linearity checking mechanism.

Figure 1:
(a) State-of-the-art MPST implementations and (b)
ocaml-mpst
methodology

Our approach.
This paper presents a library for programming MPST protocols in OCaml that solves the above challenges.
Our library,
ocaml-mpst
, allows to specify, verify and implement
MPST protocols in a single language, OCaml. Specifically, we address
(C1)
by developing

global combinators
, an embedded DSL (EDSL) for writing global
types in OCaml. We address
(C2)
by
encoding multiparty channels into
channel
vectors
– a data structure, storing a nested sequence of binary channels. Moreover,

ocaml-mpst
verifies
statically
the linear usage of communication channels,
using OCaml’s strong typing system and supports session delegation.

The key device in our approach is the discovery that in a system with
variant and record types, checking compatibility of local types
coincides with existence of least upper bound
w.r.t. subtyping relation. This realisation enables a fully
static MPST implementation, i.e., static checking not only on local but also on global types in a general purpose language.

Programming with
ocaml-mpst
(Fig.
1
(b))
closely follows the “top-down” methodology of MPST, but
differs from the traditional MPST framework in
Fig.
1
(a).
To use our library, a programmer specifies the global protocol with a set of global combinators.
The OCaml typechecker verifies correctness of the global protocol
and infers local types from global combinators.
A developer implements the endpoint processes using our
ocaml-mpst
API. Finally, the OCaml type checker verifies that the API is used according to the inferred type.

The benefits of
ocaml-mpst
are that it is (1)
lightweight
– it does
not depend on any external code-generation mechanism, verification of
global protocols is reduced to typability of global combinators; (2)

fully-static
– our embedding integrates with recent techniques for static checking of binary session types and linearly-typed lists
[
33
,
31
]
, which we adopt to
implement multiparty session channels and session delegation;
(3)
usable
– we can auto-detect and correct protocol violations in
the program, guided by OCaml programming environments like Merlin
[
5
]
;
(4)
extensible
– while most MPST implementations rely on a
nominal typing, we embed session types in OCaml’s
structural

types, and preserve session subtyping
[
23
]
;
and (5)
expressive
– we can
type strictly more processes than
[
55
]

(see §
7
).

Contributions.
Contributions and the outline of the paper are as follows:

§
2

gives an overview of programming with
ocaml-mpst
, a
library in OCaml for specification, verification and
implementations of communication protocols.

§
3

formalises global combinators, presents their typing
system, and proves a
sound realisability of
global combinator
, i.e.
a set of local types
inferred from a global combinator can type
a channel which embeds a set of endpoint behaviours as OCaml data structures.

§
4

discusses the design and implementation of global combinators.

§
5

summarises
the
ocaml-mpst
communication library and explains how we utilise
advanced features/libraries in OCaml to enable dynamic/static linearity checking on channels.

§
6

evaluates
ocaml-mpst
.
We compare
ocaml-mpst
with several different implementations and
demonstrate the expressiveness of
ocaml-mpst
by showing implementations
of MPST examples, as well as a variety of real-world protocols.
We demonstrate our library can interoperate with existing libraries and services, namely we implement DNS (Domain Name Service) and the OAuth (Open Authentication) protocols on top of existing libraries.

We discuss related work in §
7
and
conclude with future work in §
8
.
Full proofs, omitted definitions and examples can be found in
Appendix.
Our implementation,
ocaml-mpst
is available at
https://github.com/keigoi/ocaml-mpst

including benchmark programs and results.

2
Overview of OCaml Programming with Global Combinators

This section gives an overview of multiparty
session programming in
ocaml-mpst
by examples. It starts from
declaration of global combinators, followed by endpoint
implementations. We also demonstrate how
errors can be reported by an OCaml programming environment like Merlin
[
5
]
.
In the end of this section, we show the syntax of global combinators and the constructs
of
ocaml-mpst
API in Fig.
9
.
The detailed explanation of the implementations of the constructs
is deferred to §
4
.

From global combinators to communication programs.
We illustrate

global combinators
starting from a simple authentication protocol
(based on OAuth 2.0
[
25
]
).
A full version of the protocol is implemented and discussed in
§
6
. Fig.
2
shows the complete OCaml implementation of the protocol,
from the protocol specification (using global combinators)
to the endpoint implementations (using
ocaml-mpst
API).

Figure 2:
Global protocol and local implementations for OAuth protocol
2
2

2

We use a simplified syntax that support the in-built communication transport of Ocaml. For the full syntax of the library that is parametric on the transport, see the
repository
.

The protocol consists of three parties, a service

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}
, a client
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}
, and an authenticator
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{a}}}}
.
The interactions between the parties (hereafter also called
roles
) proceed as follows:
(1) the service
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}
sends to the client

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}
a
login
message containing a greeting (of type

string
); (2) the client then continues by sending its
password (
pwd
) (of type
string
) to the authenticator

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{a}}}}
; and (3) finally the authenticator
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{a}}}}
notifies
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}
, by sending an
auth
message (of type
bool
), whether the client
access is authorised.

The global protocol
oAuth
in Line
1
is specified using two global combinators,
-->
and

finish
.
The former represents a
point-to-point communication between two roles, while the latter signals the end of a protocol.
The operator
@@
is a right-associative function application operator to eliminate parentheses, i.e.,
(
c

-->

a
)

pwd

@@

\mathit{exp}
is equivalent to

(
c

-->

a
)

pwd

(
\mathit{exp}
)
, where
-->
works as a four-ary function
which takes roles
c
and
a
and label
pwd
and continuation
\mathit{exp}
.
We assume that
login
,

pwd
and
auth
are predefined by the user as
label objects
with their
payload types
of
string
,
string
and

bool
, respectively
3
3

3

To be precise, the labels are
polymorphic
on their payload types which are instantiated at the point where they are used.

.
Similarly,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{a}}}}

are predefined
role objects
.
We elaborate on how to define these custom labels and roles in §
4
.

The execution of the
oAuth
expression returns a tuple of three
channel vectors
– one for each role in the global combinator.
Each element of the tuple can be extracted using an index, encoded in role objects (
c
,
s
, and
a
).
Intuitively, the role object
c
stores a functional pointer that points to the first element of the tuple,
s
points to the second, and
a
to the third element.
The types of the extracted channel vectors reflect the local behaviour that each role, specified in the protocol, should implement.
Channel vectors are objects that hide the
actual bare communication channels
shared between every two communicating processes.

Lines
2
–
20

present the implementations for all three processes specified in the global protocol.
We explain the implementation for the client –
cliThread
(Lines
2
–
6
).
Other processes are similarly implemented.
Line
3
extracts the channel vector that encapsulates the behaviour of the
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}
lient,
i.e the first element of
oAuth
.
This is done by using the function
get_ch
(provided by our library) applied to the role object
c
and the expression
oAuth
.

Our library provides two main communication primitives, namely
send
and
recv
.
To statically check communication structures using types,
we exploit OCaml’s
structural
types of objects and polymorphic variants
(rather than their nominal counterparts of records and ordinary variants).
In Line
4
,

ch
#
role_S
is an invocation of method
role_S
on an object
ch
.
The
recv
primitive waits on a
bare channel
returned by the method invocation.
The returned value is matched against a variant tag indicating the input label
‘
login
with
the pair of the payload value
x
and a continuation
ch
(shadowing the previous usage of
ch
).
Then, on Line
5
, two method calls on
ch
are performed, e.g
ch
#
role_A
#
pwd
, which extract a communication channel for sending a password (
pwd
) to the

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{a}}}}
uthenticator. This channel is passed
to the
send
primitive, along with the payload value
"pass"
.
Then,
let
rebinds the name
ch
to the continuation returned by
send
and on
Line
6
the channel is closed.
Each operation is guided by the host OCaml type system, via
channel vector type
.
For example, the
c
lient channel
ch
extracted in Line
3
has a channel vector type (inferred by OCaml type checker)

<
role_S
:

[‘
login

of

string

*

t
]

inp
>

which denote reception (suffixed by
inp
) from server of a
login
label, then continuing to
t
, where

t
is
<
role_A
:
<
pwd
:
(
string
,
close
)

out
>
>

denoting sending (
out
) to authenticator of a
pwd
label, followed by closing.
Note that the type
<
f
:

t
>
denotes an OCaml object with a
field
f
of type
t
;
[‘
m

of

t
]
is a (polymorphic)
variant type having a tag
m
of type
t
.
Finally, in Lines
24
–
27
all processes are started in new threads.

Figure 5:
Extended
oAuth
protocols

On the expressiveness of well-typed global protocols.

Fig.
5
shows two global protocols that extend
oAuth
with new behaviours.
In Fig.
5
, the global combinator
choice_at
specifies a branching
behaviour at role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}
.
In the first case (Line
3
),
the protocol proceeds with
protocol
oAuth
. In the second case (Line
5
) the service sends
cancel
,
to the client, and the client sends a
quit
message to the authenticator.
The deciding role,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}
, is explicit in each branch.
The choice combinator requires a user-defined

(
to_s

login_cancel
)
(Line
2
) that specifies
concatenation of two objects for sending in branches. Its implementation
is straightforward (see §
4
). The protocol

oAuth3
in Fig.
5
reuses
oAuth2
and
further elaborates its behaviour by offering a retry option. It
demonstrates a recursive specification where the
fix
combinator binds the protocol itself to variable
repeat
.

The implementation of the corresponding client code for Fig.
5
is shown on
Fig.
8
. The code is similar as before,
but uses a pattern matching against multiple tags

‘
login
and
‘
cancel
to specify an
external choice
on the client, i.e the client can receive messages of different types
and exhibit different behaviour according to received labels. The behaviour that a role can send messages of different types, which is often referred to as an
internal choice
, is represented as an object with multiple methods.

Our implementation also preserves the subtyping relation in session types
[
23
]
, i.e the safe
replacement of a channel of more capabilities in a context where a channel of less
capabilities is expected. Session subtyping is important in practice since it ensures
backward compatibility for protocols:
a new version of a protocol does not break existing implementations.
For example, the client function in
Fig.
8
is typable under both protocols

oAuth2
and
oAuth3
since the type of the channel stipulating the behaviour for role
c
in
oAuth2
(receiving either message
‘
login
or
‘

) is a subtype of the channel for
c
in
oAuth3
(receiving
‘

,
‘

, or
‘

).

Static linearity and session delegation.

The implementations presented in Fig.
2
, as well as Fig.
8

detect linearity violations at runtime, as common in MPST implementations
[
29
,
54
]
in a non-substructural type system.
We overcome this dynamic checking issue by an alternative approach, listed in Fig.
8
.
We utilise an extension (
let
%
lin
) for linear types in OCaml
[
31
]
that statically
enforces linear usage of resources by combining the usage of
parameterised monads
[
35
,
2
,
46
]
and lenses
[
19
]
.
Our library is parameterised on the chosen approach, static or dynamic.
A few changes are made to avoid explicit handling of linear resources:
(1)
ch
in Fig.
8

refers to a
linear
resource and has to be matched against a
linear pattern
prefixed by
#
.
(2) Roles and labels are now specified as a

(

)

Our implementation is also the first to support
static

multiparty sessions delegation (the capability to pass a channel to
another endpoint): our encoding yields it for free, via existing

).

Errors in global protocol and
ocaml-mpst
endpoint
programs.

Our framework ensures that a well-typed
ocaml-mpst
program precisely implements the behaviour of its defined global protocol. Hence, if a program does not conform to its protocol, a compilation error is
reported. Fig.
10
shows the error reported when swapping the order of send and receive actions
(Lines
5
and
4
) in the client implementation in Fig.
2
.
Similarly, errors will also be reported if we misspell any of the methods

pwd
,
role_A
, or
role_C
.

Similarly, an error is reported if the global protocol is
not safe
(which corresponds to an ill-formed MPST protocols
[
16
]
) since this may lead to
unsafe

implementations. Consider Fig.
10
(b), where we modify
oAuth2

such that
s
sends a
cancel
message to

a
. This protocol (
oAuth4
) exhibits a race condition: even if all parties adhere to the specified behaviour,

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}
can send a
quit
before
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}
sends
login
, which will lead to a deadlock on
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}
.
Our definition of global combinators
prevents such ill-formed protocols, and the OCaml compiler
will report an error.
The actual error message reported in OCaml detects the mismatch between
a
and
c
,
indicating violation of the
active role
property in the MPST literature
[
16
]
–
the sender must send to the same role.

3
Formalisms and Typing for Global Combinators

This section formalises global combinators and their typing system,
along a formal correspondence between a global combinator and
channel vectors. The aim of this section is to provide
a guidance towards
descriptions of the implementations presented in
§
4
,
5
.

We first give the syntax of global combinators and channel vectors
in §
3.1
. We then propose
a typing system of global combinators in §
3.2
,
illustrating that the rules check their well-formedness.
We define derivation of channel vectors from global combinators
in §
3.3
.
The main theorem (Theorem
3.11
)
states that a well-typed global combinator always derives
a channel vector which is typable by a corresponding set of
local types, i.e. any well-typed global combinator is soundly realisable
by a tuple of well-typed channel vectors.

3.1
Global Combinators and Channel Vector Types

Global combinators
denote
a communication protocol
which describes the whole
conversation scenario of a multiparty session.

Definition 3.1

(Global combinators and channel vector types)
.

The syntax of
global combinators
, written
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}},{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}}^{\prime},..
,
are given as:

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}}\ \ {::=}\ {\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{(}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\,\rightarrow\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\texttt{)}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}}{:}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}}}}{\;\text{\Large$\mid$}\;}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{choice}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}_{i}}}\right\}_{i\in I}}{\;\text{\Large$\mid$}\;}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{fix}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}x}}}\,\texttt{-{}>}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}}}}{\;\text{\Large$\mid$}\;}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}x}}{\;\text{\Large$\mid$}\;}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{finish}}

where the syntax of
payload types

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T},\ldots
(also called
channel vector types
) is
given below:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}\quad{::=}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}{\;\text{\Large$\mid$}\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}?}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}{\;\text{\Large$\mid$}\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sharp}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}{\;\text{\Large$\mid$}\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{1}}{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\scriptstyle\times}}}{\mathinner{\cdotp\kern-1.99997pt\cdotp\kern-1.99997pt\cdotp}}{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\scriptstyle\times}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{n}}{\;\text{\Large$\mid$}\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}\,{:}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}\right\rangle_{i\in I}}{\;\text{\Large$\mid$}\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 6.99997pt}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}\right]_{i\in I}}{\;\text{\Large$\mid$}\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}{.}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}{\;\text{\Large$\mid$}\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}{\;\text{\Large$\mid$}\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\bullet}

The formal syntax of global combinators comes from Scribble

[
57
]
and corresponds to the standard global types in MPSTs
[
43
]
.
We assume a set of
participants (
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathfrak{R}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}},\cdots\}
),
and that of alphabets (
\mathbb{A}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{ok}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{cancel}}},\cdots\}
).
Communication combinator

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{(}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\,\rightarrow\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\texttt{)}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}}{:}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}}}}

states that participant
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
can
send a message of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
with label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}
to participant
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}

and that the interaction
described in
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}}
follows.
We require
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
to prevent self-sent messages.
We omit the payload type when
unit
type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\bullet}
,
and assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
is
closed
, i.e. it does not contain free recursive
variables.

Choice combinator

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{choice}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}_{i}}}\right\}_{i\in I}}

is a branching in a protocol where
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
makes a decision
(i.e. an output) on which branch
the participants will take.

Recursion

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{fix}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}x}}}\,\texttt{-{}>}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}}}}
is for recursive protocols,
assuming that variables
(
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}x}},{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}x}^{\prime}},\dots
) are guarded in the
standard way, i.e. they only occur under the communication combinator.

Termination

finish
represents session termination.
We write
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}}})
(or simply
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\in\!{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}}
)  iff, for some
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
, either
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}{\rightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}{\rightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
occurs in
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}}
.

Example 3.2
.

The global combinator
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}_{\mathrm{Auth}}}
below specifies a variant of
an authentication protocol in Fig.
5

where
T=\mathtt{string}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}
lient sends
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{auth}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}
erver, then

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}
erver replies with either
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{ok}}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{cancel}}}
.

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}_{\mathrm{Auth}}}={\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{(}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}\,\rightarrow\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}\texttt{)}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{auth}}}}{:}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\ {\bigl({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{choice}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{(}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}\,\rightarrow\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}\texttt{)}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{ok}}}}{:}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{finish}}}}{\texttt{,}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{(}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}\,\rightarrow\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}\texttt{)}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{cancel}}}}{:}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{finish}}}}\right\}}\bigr)}}\end{array}

Channel vector types
abstract behaviours of each
participant using standard data structure and channels.
We assume
labels

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}^{\prime},\ldots
range over

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathfrak{R}}\cup\mathbb{A}
.
Types
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}?}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}
denote

output
and
input channel types
,
with a value or channel of type

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
(note that the syntax includes
session delegation
).

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sharp}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}
is an
io
-type which is a subtype of both input or output types
[
53
]
.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{1}}{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\scriptstyle\times}}}{\mathinner{\cdotp\kern-1.99997pt\cdotp\kern-1.99997pt\cdotp}}{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\scriptstyle\times}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{n}}
is an
n
-ary

tuple type
.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}\,{:}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}\right\rangle_{i\in I}}
is a
record
type

where each field
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}
has type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}
for
i\in I
.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 6.99997pt}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}\right]_{i\in I}}

is a
variant type

[
53
]
where each
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}

is a possible
tag
(or
constructor
) of that type and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}
is the argument type of the tag.
In both record and variant types, we assume the fields and tags are distinct
(i.e. in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}\,{:}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}\right\rangle_{i\in I}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 6.99997pt}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}\right]_{i\in I}}
,
we assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{j}}}
for all
i\neq j
).
The symbol
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\bullet}
denotes a unit type.
Type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}
is a variable for recursion.
A
recursive type
takes an equi-recursive viewpoint,
i.e.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}{.}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}

is viewed as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}{.}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}}}\right\}}}
.
Recursion variables are guarded and payload types are
closed.

Channel vectors: Session types as record and variant types.

The execution model of MPST assumes that processes communicate
by exchanging messages over input/output (I/O) channels.
Each channel has the capability to communicate with multiple other processes.

A local session type
prescribes the local behaviour for a role in a
global protocol by assigning a type to the communication channel utilised by the role. More precisely,
a local session type specifies
the exact order and payload types for the communication actions performed on each channel (see Fig.
1
(a)).
In practice, processes communicate on a low-level
bi-directional
I/O channels (
bare
channels), which
are used for synchronisation of two (but
not
multiple) processes.
Therefore, to implement local session types in practice, a process should utilise
multiple bare channels,
preserving the order, in which such channels should be used.
We encode local session types as channel vector types, which

wrap
bare channels (represented in our setting by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}?}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sharp}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}
types) in record and variant types.
This is illustrated in the following table,
with the corresponding local session types for reference.

Behaviour

Channel vector type

Local session type
[
56
]

Selection (Output choice)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{:}\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}}}\rangle_{i\in I}\rangle}

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\oplus_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}T_{i}}}}

Branching (Input choice)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{:}{{{?}[{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}{\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 6.99997pt}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}}}]}}_{i\in I}\rangle}

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\&_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}T_{i}}}}

Recursion

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}{.}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}T}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}

Closing

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\bullet}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathbf{end}}

Intuitively, the behaviour of sending a message is represented as a record type, which stores inside its fields a bare output channel and a continuation; the input channel required when receiving a message is stored in a variant type.
Type

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{:}\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}}}\rangle_{i\in I}\rangle}

is read as: to send label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
,
(1) the channel vector should be ‘peeled off’ from the nested record by
extracting the field
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}
;
then (2) it returns a pair
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}}{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\scriptstyle\times}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}
of an output
channel and a continuation.
Type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{:}{{{?}[{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}{\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 6.99997pt}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}}}]}}_{i\in I}\rangle}

says that (1) the process extracts the value stored in the field

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
, then reads on the resulting input channel (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}?}{}
)
to receive a variant of type

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 6.99997pt}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}}}\right]_{i\in I}}
;
then, (2) the tag (constructor)
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}
of the received variant
indicates the label which
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
has sent,
and the former’s argument
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}
is the payload, and
the latter
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}
is the continuation.

The anti-symmetric structures between output types
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{:}\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}}}\rangle_{i\in I}\rangle}

and input types
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{:}{{{?}[{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}{\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 6.99997pt}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}}}]}}_{i\in I}\rangle}

(notice the placements of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}!}{}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}?}{}
symbol in these types)
come from the fact that an output is an
internal choice

where output labels are proactively chosen via projection on a record field,
while an input is an
external choice

where input labels are reactively chosen via
pattern-matching among variant constructors.

3.2
Typing Global Combinators

A key finding of our work is that compatibility of local types can be checked using
a type system with record and variant subtyping.
Before explaining how each combinator ensures compatibility of types,
we give an intuition of well-formed global protocols following
[
16
]
.

Well-formedness and choice combinator.

A well-formed global protocol ensures that a
protocol can be correctly and
safely
realised by a system of endpoint processes.
Moreover, a set of processes that follow the prescribed behaviour is
deadlock-free
.
Well-formedness imposes several restrictions on the protocol structure, notably on
choices
.
This is necessary because some protocols, such as
oAuth4
in Fig.
10
(b) (§
2
),
are unsafe or inconsistent.
More precisely, a protocol is well-formed if local types can be
generated for all of its roles, i.e the
endpoint projection

function

[
16
, Def. 3.1]
[Def.
F.3
in Appendix (§
F
)]
is defined for all roles.
Our encoding allows the well-formedness restrictions to be checked
statically
, by the OCaml typechecker.
Below, we explain the main syntactic restrictions of endpoint projection, which are imposed on
choices
and checked statically:

R1

(
active role
) in each branch of a choice,
the first interaction
is from the same sender role (
active role
) to the same receiver role (
directed output
).

R2

(
deterministic choice
) output labels from an active role are pairwise distinct (i.e., protocols are deterministic)

R3

(
mergeable
) the behaviour of a role from all branches should be mergeable, which is ensured by the following restrictions:

M1

two input choices are merged only if
(1) their sender roles are the same (
directed input
), and (2) their continuations are recursively mergeable if labels are the same.

M2

two output choices can be merged only if they are the same.

Intuitively, the conditions in
R3
ensure that a process is able to determine unambiguously
which branch of the choice has been taken by the active role, otherwise
the process should be
choice-agnostic
, i.e it should preform the same actions in all branches.
Requirement
R3
is known in the MPST literature as
recursive full merging

[
16
]
.

Typing system for global combinators.
Deriving channel vector types from a global combinator
corresponds to the
end point projection
in multiparty
session types
[
28
]
.
Projection of global protocols relies on the notion of merging (
R3
).
As a result of the encoding of local types as channel vectors with record and variants,
the
merging
relation coincides with the
least upper bound
(join) in the subtyping relation.
This key observation allows us to embed well-formed global protocols
in OCaml, and check them using the OCaml type system.

Next we give the typing system of global combinators, explaining how each of the
typing rules ensures the verification conditions
R1
-
R3
. The
typing system uses the following subtyping rules.

Definition 3.3
.

The subtyping relation
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}
is
co
inductively defined by the following rules.

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\bullet}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{\scriptsize[{\sc Osub-${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\bullet}$}]}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sharp}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{\scriptsize[{\sc Osub-OutCh}]}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{\scriptsize[{\sc Osub-Out}]}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}\,{:}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}}\right\rangle_{i\in I}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}\,{:}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}\right\rangle_{i\in I}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{\scriptsize[{\sc Osub-RcdDepth}]}}\ \ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}\ \ i\in I{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 6.99997pt}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}}\right]_{i\in I}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 6.99997pt}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}_{i}}\right]_{i\in I\cup J}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{\scriptsize[{\sc Osub-Var}]}}\ \ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}\ \ i\in I\\[5.69054pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sharp}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}?}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{\scriptsize[{\sc Osub-InpCh}]}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}?}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}?}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{\scriptsize[{\sc Osub-Inp}]}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{1}}{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\scriptstyle\times}}}{\mathinner{\cdotp\kern-1.99997pt\cdotp\kern-1.99997pt\cdotp}}{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\scriptstyle\times}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{n}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{1}}{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\scriptstyle\times}}}{\mathinner{\cdotp\kern-1.99997pt\cdotp\kern-1.99997pt\cdotp}}{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\scriptstyle\times}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{n}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{\scriptsize[{\sc Osub-Tup}]}}\ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}\ \ i\in I{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}{.}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{\scriptsize[{\sc Osub-${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu}$L}]}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}{.}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}}}\right\}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}{.}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{\scriptsize[{\sc Osub-${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu}$R}]}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}{.}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}}}\right\}}}\end{array}

Among those, the rules
[
Osub-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu}
L
]
and
[
Osub-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu}
R
]
realise equi-recursive view of types.
The only non-standard rule is
[
Osub-RcdDepth
]
which does not allow fields to be removed in the super type.
This simulates OCaml’s lack of row polymorphism where positive occurrences of objects
are not allowed to drop fields.
Note that the negative occurrences of objects in OCaml, which we use in process implementations, for example,
do have row polymorphism, which correspond to standard record subtyping:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}\,{:}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}}\right\rangle_{i\in I\cup J}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}\,{:}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}\right\rangle_{i\in I}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}\ \ i\in I\end{array}

. We use standard record subtyping, when typing processes.
Since it permits removal of fields, it precisely simulates session subtyping on outputs.
Typing rules for processes are left to
Appendix §
C.6
.

The typing rules for global combinators (Fig.
11
) are defined by the typing judgement of the form

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}}}\mathbin{:}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}

where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}
is a type environment for recursion variables (definition follows),

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathbb{R}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{{1}}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{{n}}}
is the sequence of roles which participate in
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}}
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{1}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\scriptstyle\times}}\cdots\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\scriptstyle\times}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{n}}

is a product of channel vector types
where each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}
indicates a protocol which the role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{{i}}}

must obey.
We use the product-based encoding to closely model
our our implementation and to avoid
fixing the number of roles
n
of
finish
combinator
by using
variable-length tuples

(see
Appendix §
E
).

Definition 3.4

(Global combinator typing rules)
.

A
typing context

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}
is defined by the following grammar:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}{::=}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\emptyset}{\;\text{\Large$\mid$}\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9},}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}
.
The judgement

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}}}\mathbin{:}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}

is defined by the rules in
Fig.
11
.
We say
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}}
is
typable with

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathbb{R}}
if

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}}}\mathbin{:}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}

for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}
is empty, we write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathbb{R}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}}}\mathbin{:}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}
.

The rule
[
Otg-Comm
]
states that

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{{i}}}
has
an output type

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{{j}}}}{:}\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}}}\rangle\rangle}

to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{{j}}}
with label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}
, a payload typed by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}
and
continuation typed by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}
;
a dual input type

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{{i}}}}{:}{{{?}[{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}}{\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 6.99997pt}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{j}}}}}]}}\rangle}

from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{{j}}}
and continuation typed by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{j}}
; and
the rest of the roles are unchanged.

Rule
[
Otg-Sub
]
is the key to obtain full merging using the subtyping relation, and along
with the rule
[
Otg-Choice
]
, is a key to ensure the protocol is realisable, and free of communication errors.
The rule
[
Otg-Choice
]
requires (1) role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}_{{a}}}
to have an
output type to the same destination role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
, which satisfies
R1
.
The output labels
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{\mathnormal{k}}}\}_{k\in K_{i}}
are mutually disjoint at
each branch
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}_{i}}
, and are merged into a single record, which ensures that the choice is deterministic (
R2
).
All other types stay the same, up to subtyping.
Following requirement
M1
of
R3
, a non-directed external choices are prohibited.
This is ensured by encoding the sender role of an input type as a record field,
As the two different destination role labels would result in two record types with no join, following subtyping rule
[
Osub-RcdDepth
]
,
a non-directed external choices are safely reported as a type error. Non-directed internal choices are similarly prohibited (
M2
).
On the other hand, directed external choices are allowed, as stipulated by
M1
, and ensured by the subtyping relation on variant types
[
Osub-Var
]
.
For example, the two input types
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{:}{{{?}[{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{\mathnormal{1}}}}{\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 6.99997pt}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{1}}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{1}}}}}]}}\rangle}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{:}{{{?}[{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{\mathnormal{2}}}}{\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 6.99997pt}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{2}}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{2}}}}}]}}\rangle}
can be unified as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{:}{{{?}[{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}{\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 6.99997pt}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}}}}]}}_{i\in{1,2}}\rangle}
.

The rest of the rules are standard.
Rule
[
Otg
-
fix
]
is
for recursion; it assigns the recursion variable
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}x}}
a
sequence of distinct fresh type variables in the continuation which is
later looked up by
[
Otg
-
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}x}}
]
.
In
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\mathrm{tfix}}{\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\right)}}
,
we assign a unit type if the role does not
contribute to the recursion (i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}^{\prime}}
for any

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}^{\prime}}
), or forms a recursive type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}{.}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}

otherwise.

The typing rules for global combinators

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathbb{R}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}}}\mathbin{:}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}

Example 3.5

(Typing a global combinator)
.

We show that the global combinator

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}_{\mathrm{Auth}}}={\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{(}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}\,\rightarrow\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}\texttt{)}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{auth}}}\ {\left({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{choice}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{(}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}\,\rightarrow\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}\texttt{)}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{ok}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{finish}}}}{\texttt{,}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{(}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}\,\rightarrow\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}\texttt{)}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{cancel}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{finish}}}}\right\}}\right)}}
has the following type under
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}{:}{{{?}[{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{auth}}}}{\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 6.99997pt}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}{:}\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{ok}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\bullet}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{cancel}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\bullet}}}}\rangle\rangle}}}}]}}\rangle}{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\scriptstyle\times}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}{:}\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{auth}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}{:}{{{?}[{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{ok}}}}{\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 6.99997pt}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\bullet}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{cancel}}}}{\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 6.99997pt}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\bullet}}}}]}}\rangle}}}}\rangle\rangle}

First, see that

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}_{1}}=\left({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{(}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}\,\rightarrow\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}\texttt{)}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{ok}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{finish}}}}\right)

has a typing derivation
as follows (note that we omit the payload type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
in global combinators):

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{(}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}\,\rightarrow\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}\texttt{)}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{ok}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{finish}}}}}\mathbin{:}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}{:}\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{ok}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\bullet}}}}\rangle\rangle}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\scriptstyle\times}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}{:}{{{?}[{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{ok}}}}{\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 6.99997pt}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\bullet}}}}]}}\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{finish}}}\mathbin{:}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\bullet}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\scriptstyle\times}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\bullet}}

For
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}_{2}}=\left({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{(}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}\,\rightarrow\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}\texttt{)}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{cancel}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{finish}}}}\right)
we have similar derivation.
Then,
type of role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}
(the second of the tuple) is adjusted
by
[
Otg-Sub
]
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}{:}{{{?}[{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{ok}}}}{\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 5.95006pt}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\bullet}}}}]}}\rangle}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}{:}{{{?}[{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{ok}}}}{\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 5.95006pt}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\bullet}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{cancel}}}}{\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 5.95006pt}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}{\times}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\bullet}}}}]}}\rangle}
and

Definition 3.7

(Typing rules for channel vectors)
.

Fig.
12

gives the typing rules for channel vectors and wrappers.
The typing judgement for
(1) channel vectors
has the form

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}\mathbin{:}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}
;
(2) wrappers
has the form

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}}\mathbin{:}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}H}}
where
the type for wrappers is defined as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}H}{::=}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S}}]
;
We assume that all types in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}
are closed.

{subfigure}

[t]0.99

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9},}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sharp}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\mathbin{:}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sharp}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\lx@proof@logical@and{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{\scriptsize[{\sc Definition 3.8

(Operations)
.

(1)

The
unfolding

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{unfold}^{\ast}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}\right)}}
of a recursive value is defined by
the smallest
n
such that

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{unfold}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{n}}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}\right)}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{unfold}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{n+1}}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}\right)}}
,
and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{unfold}\!\left({\cdot}\right)}}
is defined as:

\begin{array}[]{rcllrcll}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{unfold}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}}{\mathbin{.}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}}}\right)}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}}{\mathbin{.}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}}}}\right\}}}&&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{unfold}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}\right)}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}

where
f^{n+1}(x)=f(f^{n}(x))
for
n\geq 2
and
f^{1}(x)=f(x)
.

(2)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}
denotes
the
record projection
, which
projects on field
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}
of record value
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}
, defined as:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}{=}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{i}}}\right\rangle_{i\in I}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{k}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{unfold}^{\ast}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{k}}}\right)}}
,
where

\#
is left-associative, i.e.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{1}}}\#...\#{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{n}}}=((...({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{1}}})\#...)\#{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}_{\mathnormal{n}}})
.

(3)

The
i
-th projection on a tuple,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({i})}}
is defined as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{1}},\mathinner{\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{n}}}\right)}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({i})}}{=}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{i}}
for
1\leq i\leq n
.

(4)

{{\mathrm{fix}}{\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x^{\prime}}}\right)}}{=}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{()}}
; otherwise

{{\mathrm{fix}}{\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}\right)}}{=}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}}{\mathbin{.}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}}
.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s_{i}}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S_{i}}}\}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s_{i}}\in\operatorname{fn}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}\right)}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}\mathbin{:}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}

Dynamic Linearity Checking.

withabooleanflagthatissettotrueoncethechannelhasbeenused

Iflinearityisviolated

Figure 22:
Merging of channel vectors
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bigsqcup}_{i\in 1..n}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{i}}}}=(({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{1}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sqcup}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\emptyset}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{2}})\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sqcup}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\emptyset}}}\cdots\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sqcup}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\emptyset}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{n}})

Example B.2
.

Let

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}_{Cal}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{fix}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}x}}}\,\texttt{-{}>}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{choice}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{(}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}\,\rightarrow\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}\texttt{)}\,\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{loop}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}x}}}}{\texttt{,}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{(}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}\,\rightarrow\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}\texttt{)}\,\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{stop}}\ {\left({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{(}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}\,\rightarrow\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}\texttt{)}\,\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{stop}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\texttt{finish}}}}\right)}}\right\}}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{let}\mkern 2.5mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{1}}}\mkern 2.5mu{=}\mkern 2.5mu\textbf{send}\mkern 2.5mu\mkern 2.5mu{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{0}}\#{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}\#\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{loop}}\mkern 2.5mu\mkern 2.5mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{()}}}\mkern 2.5mu\mathbf{in}\mkern 2.5mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{let}\mkern 2.5mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{2}}}\mkern 2.5mu{=}\mkern 2.5mu\textbf{send}\mkern 2.5mu\mkern 2.5mu{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{1}}\#{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}\#\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{loop}}\mkern 2.5mu\mkern 2.5mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{()}}}\mkern 2.5mu\mathbf{in}\mkern 2.5mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{let}\mkern 2.5mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{3}}}\mkern 2.5mu{=}\mkern 2.5mu\textbf{send}\mkern 2.5mu\mkern 2.5mu{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{2}}\#{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}\#\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{stop}}\mkern 2.5mu\mkern 2.5mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{()}}}\mkern 2.5mu\mathbf{in}\mkern 2.5mu{}}}}}}\\
&&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{let}\mkern 2.5mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{stop}}}{({\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 6.99997pt}}}{,}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{4}}})}}}\mkern 2.5mu{=}\mkern 2.5mu\textbf{recv}\mkern 2.5mu\mkern 2.5mu{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{3}}\#\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}}}\mkern 2.5mu\mkern 2.5mu\mathbf{in}\mkern 2.5mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{letrec}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}}\!\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}}\right\rangle}{=}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}0}}}\;\mathbf{in}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}}\!\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x^{\prime}_{0}}}\right\rangle}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}0}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{match}}\mkern 2.5mu\mathbf{recv}\mkern 2.5mu{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}\#\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}}\mkern 2.5mu\mkern 2.5mu\mathbf{with}\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{loop}}}({\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 6.99997pt}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{1}}})\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}}\!\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{1}}}\right\rangle}}};\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{stop}}}({\mbox{\lx@text@underline{\hskip 6.99997pt}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{2}}})\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{let}\mkern 2.5mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{3}}}\mkern 2.5mu{=}\mkern 2.5mu\textbf{send}\mkern 2.5mu\mkern 2.5mu{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{2}}\#{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}\#\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{stop}}\mkern 2.5mu\mkern 2.5mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{()}}}\mkern 2.5mu\mathbf{in}\mkern 2.5mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}}}\right\}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e_{\tt Cal}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{let}\mkern 2.5mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{0}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x^{\prime}_{0}}}\mkern 2.5mu{=}\mkern 2.5mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{g}}_{Cal}}}\mkern 2.5mu\mathbf{in}\mkern 2.5mu{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}}\right)}}\end{array}

Then,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e_{\tt Cal}}\rightarrow\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e^{\prime}_{\tt Cal}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\mathbf{\nu}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}}}){\bigl({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{0}}}}}\right\}}}\ \big|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{letrec}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}}\!\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}}\right\rangle}{=}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}0}}}\;\mathbf{in}\;{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}0}}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x^{\prime}_{0}}}}}\right\}}})}}\bigr)}}

where

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}}{\mathbin{.}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}{=}{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{loop}}}{=}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s_{1}}}{,}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}})}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{stop}}}{=}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s_{2}}}{,}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}{=}{\left[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{stop}}}{=}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left({\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}}}}{,}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{()}}}\right)}}\right]}\right\rangle}})}}}\right\rangle}\right\rangle}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}}}{\mathbin{.}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}{=}{\left[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{loop}}}{=}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s_{1}}}}{,}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}}})}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{stop}}}{=}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s_{2}}}}{,}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}{=}{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{stop}}}{=}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}}}{,}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{()}}}\right)}}}\right\rangle}\right\rangle}})}}\right]}\right\rangle}}}\\
&&\left(={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}}}{\mathbin{.}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}{=}{\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s_{1}}}{{\mathtt{@}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{loop}}}{=}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left({{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[\ ]}}}{,}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}}}\right)}}\right]}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s_{2}}}{{\mathtt{@}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{stop}}}{=}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left({{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[\ ]}}}{,}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}}{=}{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{stop}}}{=}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}}}{,}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{()}}}\right)}}}\right\rangle}\right\rangle}}\right)}}\right]}}}\right]}\right\rangle}}}\right)\\

See that

and each time
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}
lient sends a label,
